r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Define the score of a sequence of positive integers B = (B_1, B_2, \dots, B_k) as \displaystyle \sum_{i=1}^{k-1} \gcd(B_i, B_{i+1}).
Given a sequence of positive integers A = (A_1, A_2, \dots, A_N), solve the following problem for m = 1, 2, \dots, N.

- There are 2^m - 1 non-empty subsequences of the sequence (A_1, A_2, \dots, A_m). Find the sum of the scores of all those subsequences, modulo 998244353. Two subsequences are distinguished if they are taken from different positions in the sequence, even if they coincide as sequences.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \dots A_N

Output

Print N lines. The i-th line should contain the answer for m = i.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 5 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq A_i \leq 10^5
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3
9 6 4

Sample Output 1

0
3
11

Consider the case m = 3. Here are the non-empty subsequences of (A_1, A_2, A_3) = (9, 6, 4) and their scores.

- (9): Score is 0.
- (6): Score is 0.
- (4): Score is 0.
- (9, 6): Score is \gcd(9, 6) = 3.
- (9, 4): Score is \gcd(9, 4) = 1.
- (6, 4): Score is \gcd(6, 4) = 2.
- (9, 6, 4): Score is \gcd(9, 6) + \gcd(6, 4) = 3 + 2 = 5.

Therefore, the answer for m = 3 is 0 + 0 + 0 + 3 + 1 + 2 + 5 = 11.

Sample Input 2

5
3 8 12 6 9

Sample Output 2

0
1
13
57
155

Sample Input 3

10
47718 21994 74148 76721 98917 73766 29598 59035 69293 29127

Sample Output 3

0
2
14
35
97
372
866
1859
4273
43287

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's prove recurrence more formally for final? Not needed in code, but maybe final answer only code. But need ensure no hidden issue: Subsequences distinguished by positions, but our DP counts by positions via weights 2^{i-1}. Let's derive recurrence carefully:
Let S_m = set of non-empty subsequences of prefix m. For prefix m+1, every subsequence either doesn't contain new element (same as S_m) or contains new element. If contains new element, removing it yields any subsequence of prefix m (possibly empty). If old subsequence nonempty with last selected position j, score(new) = score(old)+gcd(A_j,x); if old empty, score=0. Therefore total score over new-containing subsequences = sum_{old in S_m} score(old) + sum_{old in S_m} gcd(last(old), x). The first sum is ans_m. Thus ans_{m+1}=ans_m + (ans_m + T_m)=2ans_m+T_m. T_m = sum_{old nonempty} gcd(last,x). For each possible last position j<=m, number of subsequences of prefix m ending at j is 2^{j-1}, so T_m = sum_{j=1}^m gcd(A_j,x)2^{j-1}. Good.

Could use "multiplicative" transform to answer T(x) in O(1)? Let's derive.

C[d] = sum_{i: d|A_i} w_i. T(x)=sum_{d|x} phi[d] C[d]. This is a divisor sum transform. If we maintain F[x]? Actually T(x) = sum_{d|x} phi[d] C[d]. Let B[x]=T(x). Then B is Dirichlet convolution of phi and C? B(x)= (phi * C)(x) restricted to divisors? C[d] defined for all d. If we update C[d] for all d|x by w, then B(y) changes for all y multiples of d? For future query at y, T(y)=sum_{d|y} phi[d] C[d]. If we update C[d] for d|x, then for every y multiple of d, T(y) increases by phi[d]*w. So we could maintain T(y) directly: upon processing x, for each d|x, for each y multiple of d, T[y]+=phi[d]*w. That would be sum_{d|x} M/d, worse maybe for large x? But if x has many divisors and M=1e5, maybe not.

Alternative maintain G[a] = sum_i gcd(a,A_i) w_i? Then query T(x)=G[x] O(1). Update with value x: for all a=1..M, G[a] += gcd(a,x)*w. Too expensive O(M) per update.

Implementation plan:
import sys
MOD=998244353
def main():
    data=list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data: return
    N=data[0]; A=data[1:]
    M=max(A) if A else 0
    # phi sieve
    phi=list(range(M+1))
    for i in range(2,M+1):
        if phi[i]==i:
            for j in range(i,M+1,i):
                phi[j]-=phi[j]//i
    # divisors
    divs=[[] for _ in range(M+1)]
    for d in range(1,M+1):
        for x in range(d,M+1,d):
            divs[x].append(d)
    C=[0]*(M+1)
    ans=0
    w=1
    out=[]
    divs_l=divs; phi_l=phi; C_l=C; MOD_l=MOD
    for x in A:
        t=0
        for d in divs_l[x]:
            cd=C_l[d]
            t += phi_l[d] * cd
            cd += w
            if cd >= MOD_l:
                cd -= MOD_l
            C_l[d]=cd
        ans = (ans * 2 + t) % MOD_l
        out.append(str(ans))
        w += w
        if w >= MOD_l:
            w -= MOD_l
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))
if __name__=="__main__": main()

Code:
def main():
    import sys
    MOD = 998244353
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N = data[0]
    M = 0
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        v = data[i]
        if v > M:
            M = v
    # phi
    phi = list(range(M+1))
    for i in range(2, M+1):
        if phi[i] == i:
            for j in range(i, M+1, i):
                phi[j] -= phi[j] // i
    # divisors
    divs = [[] for _ in range(M+1)]
    for d in range(1, M+1):
        for x in range(d, M+1, d):
            divs[x].append(d)
    C = [0]*(M+1)
    ans = 0
    w = 1
    out = []
    append = out.append
    divs_l = divs; phi_l = phi; C_l = C; MOD_l = MOD
    for idx in range(1, N+1):
        x = data[idx]
        t = 0
        for d in divs_l[x]:
            cd = C_l[d]
            t += phi_l[d] * cd
            cd += w
            if cd >= MOD_l:
                cd -= MOD_l
            C_l[d] = cd
        ans = (ans * 2 + t) % MOD_l
        append(str(ans))
        w += w
        if w >= MOD_l:
            w -= MOD_l
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

We need T_m for each m. Suppose process in blocks of size B. For each block, before block we have C[d] for all previous positions. For each position m in block, T_m = sum_{d|A_m} phi[d] C[d] (previous positions) + sum_{i in same block, i<m} gcd(A_i,A_m)2^{i-1}. The first part can be computed by divisor enumeration per position: O(B*tau). The second part within block can be computed by O(B^2) gcd or by building local C for block and querying? This can be done by iterating d and multiples over value range M: O(M log M) per block. If block size B, number blocks N/B, total O((N/B)*M log M + N*tau? first part + B^2 per block). Current O(N*tau) ~64M. Block approach:
- For each block, compute contributions from previous positions to T_m: O(B*tau) per block -> O(N*tau) still.
- Within block O(B^2) per block -> O(N*B).
- Update C after block by batch: O(M log M) per block -> O((N/B)*M log M). If B ~ sqrt(M log M) ~ sqrt(1.2M?) Actually M log M ~1.2e6. N=5e5. If B=1000, blocks=500, update cost 500*1.2e6=600M too high. If B=5000, blocks=100, update 120M, within block 2.5e9 too high. Not good.

Could use "sqrt decomposition on values" to reduce query cost for numbers with many divisors. Let's examine. Values with many divisors are few. We can precompute for heavy values? This changes T(x) for all x that are multiples of d. For heavy x, we could update H[x] += w * gcd(v,x) for all heavy x? Let's think.

We want to answer T(x) quickly. Let choose a threshold T. For "heavy" values x with tau(x) > B, we can maintain H[x] = T(x) directly. When processing a new value v with weight w, for every heavy x, H[x] += gcd(v,x)*w. If number of heavy values is small, this update cost small. For light x, query by enumerating divisors (tau <= B). Also need update C[d] for all d|v for light queries? If we maintain H for heavy, do we still need C[d]? For light query, T(x)=sum_{d|x} phi[d]C[d], so yes need C[d]. Updating C[d] for d|v costs tau(v). But maybe we can avoid updating C[d] for divisors that are only needed by light queries? All divisors. But tau(v) max 128 anyway; not huge. Heavy-light can reduce query cost for heavy values but update cost remains. Since max tau 128, not needed. But maybe if we maintain H for heavy, query O(1) for heavy, update O(#heavy) plus C update. #heavy maybe small. But current query+update both tau; max 128. Not worth complexity.

Could choose to precompute T(x) for all x after each update? no.

Since value range small, maybe we can process all positions and compute T_m using divisor lists and prefix sums over positions for each divisor. For each divisor d, we need P_d(m-1) = sum_{i<m, d|A_i} 2^{i-1}. We can precompute for each d a list of positions where d|A_i and prefix weights. Then for each m, T_m = sum_{d|A_m} phi[d] * P_d(m-1). If we precompute for each d a Fenwick? But since queries are in increasing m, we can maintain pointer for each d? Actually as m increases, P_d(m-1) is prefix sum up to m-1. We can precompute arrays `pref[d][k]` for positions in list_d. Then for query m, for each d|A_m, binary search in list_d to find last position <m, get prefix. Complexity O(N*tau*log freq). Current O(N*tau) with no log. Current better.

Could use "offline by divisor" to compute T_m for all m by iterating d and adding phi[d]*P_d(m-1) to T_m for all m with d|A_m. For each d, we can scan positions and maintain prefix sum; for each m where d|A_m, add phi[d]*prefix to T_m. This is exactly current but maybe can be optimized by iterating over d and multiples:
Initialize T=[0]*(N+1)
For d in 1..M:
    pref=0
    For i in 1..N:
        if A_i % d ==0:
            # update T_i? Need add phi[d]*pref to T_i before updating pref with current weight?
            if d|A_i:
                T[i] += phi[d]*pref
                pref += 2^{i-1}
But this loops d*N = 1e5*5e5 =5e10 impossible. Current loops only over divisors of A_i, total N*tau ~64M. Better.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with "w += w; if w >= MOD_l: w -= MOD_l" and if w+w >= MOD but
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return

    N = data[0]

    M = 0
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        if data[i] > M:
            M = data[i]

    # Euler phi
    phi = list(range(M + 1))
    for i in range(2, M + 1):
        if phi[i] == i:
            for j in range(i, M + 1, i):
                phi[j] -= phi[j] // i

    # divisors of each number up to M
    divs = [[] for _ in range(M + 1)]
    for d in range(1, M + 1):
        for x in range(d, M + 1, d):
            divs[x].append(d)

    C = [0] * (M + 1)

    ans = 0
    w = 1  # 2^(position-1) mod MOD

    out = []
    append = out.append

    divs_l = divs
    phi_l = phi
    C_l = C
    MOD_l = MOD

    for idx in range(1, N + 1):
        x = data[idx]
        t = 0

        for d in divs_l[x]:
            cd = C_l[d]
            t += phi_l[d] * cd

            cd += w
            if cd >= MOD_l:
                cd -= MOD_l
            C_l[d] = cd

        ans = (ans * 2 + t) % MOD_l
        append(str(ans))

        w += w
        if w >= MOD_l:
            w -= MOD_l

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
